_source line 4_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Let me start by understanding the task:

1. Fix statistical numbers in several .tex files (σ, z, p values)
2. Calculate a permutation test p-value
3. Add permutation test description to appendix.tex

Let me first calculate the permutation test, then find and modify all the files.

Permutation test calculation:
- 8 values: {57, 52.5, 55, 57, 54, 52.5, 58, 58.5}
- All C(8,2) = 28 combinations of 2 elements
- Calculate mean of each combination


- p = (number of combinations with mean ≥ 58.25) / 28

Now I'm working through all 28 pairwise combinations to find their means and count how many meet the threshold of 58.25 or higher. Continuing through the remaining pairs and finding that only (6,7) has a mean at or above 58.25.
```

{
  "command": "python3 -c \"\nfrom itertools import combinations\nvals = [57, 52.5, 55, 57, 54, 52.5, 58, 58.5]\ncombos = list(combinations(vals, 2))\nprint(f'Total combinations: {len(combos)}')\ncount = 0\nfor c in combos:\n    m = sum(c)/2\n    if m >= 58.25:\n        count += 1\n        print(f'  {c} -> mean={m}')\nprint(f'Count >= 58.25: {count}')\nprint(f'p = {count}/{len(combos)} = {count/len(combos):.4f}')\n\"\n"
}
```

1	\section{Avoidance Source Diagnosis}\label{sec:avoidance_source}
2	
3	Fine-tuning on collision-free demonstrations produces genuine collision avoidance: LoRA-adapted Pi0.5 raises CAR by ${\sim}$28 percentage points with zero frozen episodes. Does SDF geometric supervision contribute additional avoidance beyond what demonstrations provide? We compare LoRA-only, LoRA+SDF ($\lambda = 0.005$), and LoRA+SDF ($\lambda = 0.01$) on Pi0.5, all evaluated at the 30K-step checkpoint.
4	
5	\begin{table}[t]
6	\centering
7	\caption{Avoidance source attribution on SafeLIBERO. SDF at $\lambda \leq 0.01$ adds no measurable CAR beyond LoRA-only ($p > 0.1$); $\lambda = 0.1$ shows a marginal signal ($z = 1.85$, $p \approx 0.032$, $n{=}2$) that requires additional seeds. All fine-tuned models exhibit 0\% frozen rate and displacement $>$\,0.37\,m.}
8	\label{tab:avoidance_source}
9	\vspace{2pt}
10	\small
11	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
12	\toprule
13	Method & Seeds & TSR (\%) & CAR (\%) & EE Disp (m) \\
14	\midrule
15	Pi0.5 baseline & 3 & 74.7\,$\pm$\,1.5 & 26.8\,$\pm$\,1.6 & 0.367 \\
16	\midrule
17	LoRA-only (no SDF) & 3 & 49.7\,$\pm$\,3.2 & 54.83\,$\pm$\,2.25 & 0.391 \\
18	LoRA+SDF $\lambda{=}0.005$ & 3 & 54.0\,$\pm$\,3.0 & 54.5\,$\pm$\,2.29 & 0.387 \\
19	LoRA+SDF $\lambda{=}0.01$ & 3 & 53.8\,$\pm$\,1.5 & 56.33\,$\pm$\,1.61 & 0.389 \\
20	LoRA+SDF $\lambda{=}0.1$\textsuperscript{$\dagger$} & 2 & 48.0\,$\pm$\,1.4 & 58.25\,$\pm$\,0.35 & 0.394 \\
21	\bottomrule
22	\end{tabular}
23	\vspace{1pt}
24	{\footnotesize \textsuperscript{$\dagger$}30K checkpoint, two of three seeds; third seed in progress.}
25	\end{table}
26	
27	Table~\ref{tab:avoidance_source} shows that collision-free demonstrations alone raise CAR by ${\sim}$28 percentage points (26.8\% $\to$ 54.83\%) with zero frozen episodes and displacement above 0.37\,m, confirming genuine avoidance. Adding SDF supervision produces no measurable benefit: LoRA-only CAR (54.83\%) is within 0.25\,pp of $\lambda = 0.005$ and statistically indistinguishable from $\lambda = 0.01$ (56.33\%, $p > 0.1$; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_null}). A Phase~2 ablation ($\lambda = 0$ after step 5K) yields CAR\,=\,57.5\%\footnote{Single seed (s2); computational constraints precluded additional seeds for this ablation.}, confirming that SDF gradients produce no lasting behavioral effect. The per-task CAR ordering is preserved across all conditions (chocolate\_pudding highest, bbq\_sauce lowest) and invariant to $\lambda$ across a 20$\times$ range, indicating that avoidance patterns reflect the demonstration distribution rather than geometric supervision.
28	
29	\paragraph{CAR--TSR trade-off.}\label{sec:tsr_collapse}
30	Fine-tuning degrades TSR by ${\sim}$21--25\,pp (74.7\% $\to$ 49.7--54.0\%), reflecting distributional shift from the pre-trained prior rather than learned paralysis (all models retain $>$\,0.37\,m displacement and 0\% frozen episodes). The SSR/TSR ratio increases from 0.33 (baseline) to 0.51--0.57 (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_ssr}), confirming that among task-completing episodes, a larger fraction avoids collisions after fine-tuning.
31	
32	\paragraph{Dose-response at higher loss weights.}\label{sec:lambda_high}
33	Increasing $\lambda$ by 20$\times$ to 0.1 yields CAR\,=\,58.25\%\,$\pm$\,0.35 at the 30K checkpoint (two of three seeds), above the null distribution (mean 54.67\%, $\sigma = 1.94$, $z = 1.85$, $p \approx 0.032$). This departure is suggestive but inconclusive at $n = 2$. A stepwise ablation across six checkpoints (16K--28K) shows no sustained trend (mean 55.3\%, within 0.3$\sigma$ of the null; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_stepwise}), and per-task CAR ordering at 30K remains identical to all other conditions (Figure~\ref{fig:lambda_car}). The response is thus dose-dependent: SDF is fully redundant at $\lambda \leq 0.01$ ($p > 0.1$), with a marginal signal at $\lambda = 0.1$ whose modest effect size (${\sim}$3.6\,pp) and limited sample preclude strong conclusions. Even at elevated $\lambda$, avoidance patterns remain driven by demonstration distribution rather than geometric supervision.
34	
35	\begin{figure}[t]
36	\centering
37	\includegraphics[width=0.85\linewidth]{fig5_lambda_curve}
38	\caption{Effect of SDF loss weight $\lambda$ on CAR and TSR. $\lambda = 0$: LoRA-only. Low $\lambda$ ($\leq 0.01$) remains within the null band; $\lambda = 0.1$ (58.25\%, 2 seeds) shows a marginal departure ($p \approx 0.032$) whose per-task pattern still matches the demonstration distribution.}
39	\label{fig:lambda_car}
40	\end{figure}
```

_source line 20 · 2026-05-27T17:08:26.491Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_011avqzZmeHa8vrb3Q24GzBD`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models report high Collision Avoidance Rates (CAR) on safety benchmarks, yet we show these metrics are contaminated by action collapse.
3	On SafeLIBERO, an SDF-augmented Pi0 achieves 91.5\% CAR while 99.6\% of its safe episodes exhibit end-effector displacement below 0.1\,mm---the robot is frozen, not safe.
4	We present displacement-filtered CAR (dfCAR), which excludes sub-threshold episodes, as a corrective metric; the bimodal displacement distribution makes it robust across a $[2, 200]$\,mm threshold range.
5	Our analysis further reveals that end-effector-only Signed Distance Field (SDF) supervision is neither necessary nor structurally sufficient for collision avoidance.
6	It is counterproductive at inference time: three ground-truth oracle variants all reduce dfCAR below unrefined baselines, as SDF gradients repelling the end-effector drive arm links into obstacles.
7	Its contribution during training is dose-dependent: LoRA-only fine-tuning raises dfCAR from 26.8\% to 54.83\%$\,\pm\,$2.25, matching SDF-augmented training at $\lambda{=}0.005$ (54.5\%$\,\pm\,$2.29) and $\lambda{=}0.01$ (56.33\%$\,\pm\,$1.61); at $\lambda{=}0.1$, two of three seeds show a marginal signal (58.25\%$\,\pm\,$0.35, $p{\approx}0.032$) whose per-task avoidance pattern still mirrors the demonstration distribution, requiring further investigation.
8	Per-task avoidance patterns remain identical across all conditions, indicating that collision avoidance arises from collision-free demonstration data rather than geometric supervision.
9	Displacement filtering is necessary for meaningful VLA safety evaluation; EE-only geometric supervision can be neither credited with nor expected to deliver collision avoidance.
10	\end{abstract}
```

</details>

      "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0515/projects/diff_collision_sdf/docs/paper/abstract.tex",
      "content": "\\begin{abstract}\nVision-Language-Action (VLA) models report high Collision Avoidance Rates (CAR) on safety benchmarks, yet we show these metrics are contaminated by action collapse.\nOn SafeLIBERO, an SDF-augmented Pi0 achieves 91.5\\% CAR while 99.6\\% of its safe episodes exhibit end-effector displacement below 0.1\\,mm---the robot is frozen, not safe.\nWe present displacement-filtered CAR (dfCAR), which excludes sub-threshold episodes, as a corrective metric; the bimodal displacement distribution makes it robust across a $[2, 200]$\\,mm threshold range.\nOur analysis further reveals that end-effector-only Signed Distance Field (SDF) supervision is neither necessary nor structurally sufficient for collision avoidance.\nIt is counterproductive at inference time: three ground-truth oracle variants all reduce dfCAR below unrefined baselines, as SDF gradients repelling the end-effector drive arm links into obstacles.\nIts contribution during training is dose-dependent: LoRA-only fine-tuning raises dfCAR from 26.8\\% to 54.83\\%$\\,\\pm\\,$2.25, matching SDF-augmented training at $\\lambda{=}0.005$ (54.5\\%$\\,\\pm\\,$2.29) and $\\lambda{=}0.01$ (56.33\\%$\\,\\pm\\,$1.61); at $\\lambda{=}0.1$, two of three seeds show a marginal signal (58.25\\%$\\,\\pm\\,$0.35, $p{\\approx}0.032$) whose per-task avoidance pattern still mirrors the demonstration distribution, requiring further investigation.\nPer-task avoidance patterns remain identical across all conditions, indicating that collision avoidance arises from collision-free demonstration data rather than geometric supervision.\nDisplacement filtering is necessary for meaningful VLA safety evaluation; EE-only geometric supervision can be neither credited with nor expected to deliver collision avoidance.\n\\end{abstract}",
      "numLines": 10,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 10
    }
  }
}

```text
1	\section{Introduction}\label{sec:intro}
2	
3	Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models must avoid collisions to operate safely in physical environments~\cite{li2026vlasafety}. The SafeLIBERO benchmark~\cite{hu2025vlsa} provides the first systematic protocol for measuring collision avoidance in VLA manipulation, and recent methods, including SDF-augmented training~\cite{zhang2025safevla}, constraint-based planning~\cite{yang2025cape}, and collision-free demonstrations~\cite{cofreeevla2026}, report high Collision Avoidance Rate (CAR). Our SDF-augmented Pi0~\cite{black2024pi0} reaches 91.5\% CAR across 800 episodes. The problem appears close to solved.
4	
5	\begin{figure}[t]
6	\centering
7	\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{fig1_teaser}
8	\caption{The fake safety problem in VLA collision avoidance evaluation. Standard CAR classifies a frozen robot as safe because it produces zero collisions (left). dfCAR filters out action-collapsed episodes where displacement falls below $\tau_d$, revealing that Pi0+A1's 91.5\% safety score drops to ${\approx}$\,0\% (center). Pi0.5 exhibits genuine avoidance with active motion above 288\,mm in all safe episodes, and dfCAR equals raw CAR (right).}
9	\label{fig:teaser}
10	\end{figure}
11	
12	It is not (Figure~\ref{fig:teaser}). Of the 732 collision-free episodes, 99.6\% exhibit displacement below 0.1\,mm; TSR is 0\% across all 800 episodes. The robot is frozen, not safe. The same pattern holds across all Pi0 configurations (Section~\ref{sec:fake_safety}). We term this \emph{fake safety}: \emph{action collapse} (near-zero actions producing zero collisions) renders reported CAR illusory, and standard evaluation cannot distinguish inaction from deliberate avoidance~\cite{karwowski2024goodhart}.
13	
14	We present a systematic investigation of this problem across the Pi0 and Pi0.5~\cite{black2024pi0} model families on SafeLIBERO, spanning 100+ experiments and 6000+ evaluation episodes. Three findings emerge.
15	
16	\textbf{Fake safety is pervasive.} We introduce displacement-filtered CAR (dfCAR), which excludes episodes below a displacement threshold $\tau_d$. dfCAR collapses Pi0+A1 from 91.5\% to ${\approx}$\,0\% (3/732 safe episodes survive). The bimodal displacement distribution makes $\tau_d$ robust across five orders of magnitude. Pi0.5 validates the metric: TSR\,=\,74.7\%, CAR\,=\,26.8\%, with every safe episode above 288\,mm displacement and dfCAR equal to raw CAR (Section~\ref{sec:fake_safety}).
17	
18	\textbf{EE-only SDF refinement is structurally counterproductive.} Three ground-truth oracle variants all achieve \emph{lower} CAR than unrefined baselines (73.1--76.9\% vs.\ 82--83\%). The SDF gradient repels the EE but rotates arm links into obstacle volumes (Section~\ref{sec:ee_only}).
19	
20	\textbf{Collision-free demonstrations are sufficient for collision avoidance.} LoRA fine-tuning on Pi0.5 raises CAR by ${\sim}$28 points (26.8\% $\to$ 54.83\%) with zero frozen episodes; adding SDF supervision at low loss weights ($\lambda \in \{0.005, 0.01\}$) produces no measurable benefit and at high $\lambda = 0.1$ yields only a marginal, inconclusive signal ($p \approx 0.032$, $n = 2$); the per-task avoidance pattern is identical with and without geometric supervision, indicating that the collision-free demonstration distribution, not EE-only SDF supervision, accounts for all observed avoidance learning on this benchmark (Section~\ref{sec:avoidance_source}).
21	
22	Our contributions are:
23	\begin{itemize}
24	    \item \textbf{dfCAR metric:} displacement-filtered CAR that separates genuine avoidance from action collapse, robust across $\tau_d \in [2, 200]$\,mm.
25	    \item \textbf{Fake safety diagnosis:} 100+ experiments across Pi0/Pi0.5 showing $>$99\% of Pi0 safe episodes are action-collapse artifacts.
26	    \item \textbf{EE-only SDF is counterproductive:} ground-truth oracle refinement \emph{reduces} CAR below unrefined baselines by redistributing collisions to unmonitored arm links.
27	    \item \textbf{Demonstration sufficiency:} on SafeLIBERO, collision-free demonstrations account for all measured CAR gains (${\sim}$28\,pp); EE-only SDF at low $\lambda$ ($\leq 0.01$) adds nothing, and at high $\lambda = 0.1$ shows only a marginal, inconclusive signal ($n = 2$) whose per-task pattern still matches the demonstration distribution.
28	\end{itemize}
```

```text
1	\section{Discussion and Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
2	
3	Across 100+ experiments and 6000+ evaluation episodes on two VLA model families, three findings converge: current VLA safety evaluation is structurally unreliable. Action collapse renders reported CAR illusory (\S\ref{sec:dfcar}); EE-only oracle refinement is counterproductive (\S\ref{sec:ee_only}); and collision-free demonstrations, not geometric supervision, account for all measured avoidance gains (\S\ref{sec:avoidance_source}).
4	
5	\paragraph{Why EE-only SDF fails.}
6	Two independent causes explain the null result. \textit{(i)~Demonstration data subsumes the SDF signal.} Collision-free demonstrations implicitly encode spatial avoidance; the ${\sim}$28\,pp CAR gain arises entirely from this distributional shift, with per-task ordering invariant across all SDF conditions (\S\ref{sec:avoidance_source}). At low $\lambda$, SDF is redundant; at $\lambda = 0.1$, the marginal signal preserves the same demonstration-driven pattern. \textit{(ii)~EE monitoring cannot prevent full-body collisions.} The SDF gradient acts on a single point while collisions occur along intermediate links. Oracle experiments confirm this: ground-truth SDF refinement \emph{reduces} CAR by 6--10\,pp below unrefined baselines (\S\ref{sec:ee_only}), because pushing the EE away rotates unmonitored links through obstacles. These levels are independent; effective geometric safety requires both full-body supervision and training signals beyond demonstrations.
7	
8	\paragraph{Implications for evaluation.}
9	dfCAR corrects action-collapse contamination using only EE position logging, already available in most evaluation pipelines. The bimodal displacement distribution makes $\tau_d$ robust across five orders of magnitude. We recommend reporting both CAR and dfCAR; the gap quantifies contamination. This contamination spans a 4000$\times$ parameter range (Pi0 Full-FT ${\sim}$2.5B to Pi0+A1 ${\sim}$658K trainable), confirming it is a structural consequence of evaluating avoidance without verifying motion. More broadly, safety may need to operate at the planning or joint-space constraint layer, since any EE-centric loss inherits the same blind spot.
10	
11	\paragraph{Limitations.}
12	Our evaluation covers one benchmark suite (SafeLIBERO, four tasks) and two model families (Pi0, Pi0.5). The tabletop setting with fixed-base manipulator and known obstacles may not generalize to mobile platforms and cluttered scenes. Oracle experiments used a largely frozen model (Pi0+A1, TSR${\approx}$0\%); testing on an actively moving policy would better isolate the structural limitation. The $\lambda = 0.1$ condition has $n = 2$ seeds; the marginal departure ($p \approx 0.032$) is inconclusive. Full-body SDF remains untested. Fine-tuning degrades TSR by ${\sim}$21--25\,pp, yet SSR/TSR increases from 0.33 to 0.51--0.57 (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_ssr}), ruling out reduced exposure as the sole explanation.
13	
14	\paragraph{Future work.}
15	Three directions follow: (1) full-body SDF supervision along the kinematic chain; (2) cross-architecture evaluation on OpenVLA, RT-2, and other VLA families; (3) planning-layer safety constraints in joint space rather than EE space.
16	
17	\paragraph{Broader impact.}
18	Contaminated CAR scores allow models with no genuine avoidance to pass safety benchmarks. dfCAR exposes this gap with minimal changes to existing protocols.
19	
```

</details>

1	\section{Displacement Distribution}
2	\label{sec:appendix_displacement}
3	
4	\begin{figure}[h]
5	\centering
6	\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{fig2_ee_displacement_distribution}
7	\caption{Displacement distribution across evaluation episodes. Pi0-family models (blue) cluster below 1\,$\mu$m, while Pi0.5 episodes (orange) exceed 200\,mm. The five-order-of-magnitude gap makes $\tau_d$ robust to any value in $[2, 200]$\,mm.}
8	\label{fig:displacement}
9	\end{figure}
10	
11	\section{Displacement Threshold Sensitivity Analysis}
12	\label{sec:appendix_threshold}
13	
14	To validate the robustness of our displacement threshold $\tau_d$, we evaluate the frozen rate across four orders of magnitude. Table~\ref{tab:threshold_sensitivity} shows that the fake safety pattern persists across all reasonable threshold choices: A1-LoRA exhibits near-total action collapse regardless of threshold, while Pi0.5+A1 maintains 0\% frozen rate even at the most conservative setting.
15	
16	\begin{table}[h]
17	\centering
18	\caption{Frozen rate (\% of episodes with $d_\mathrm{max} < \tau_d$) across threshold values.}
19	\label{tab:threshold_sensitivity}
20	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
21	\toprule
22	$\tau_d$ & A1-LoRA (Pi0) & Pi0.5 Baseline & Pi0.5+A1 (SDF aux) \\
23	\midrule
24	0.001 mm (1\,$\mu$m) & 73.9 & 0.0 & 0.0 \\
25	0.1 mm  & 99.6 & 0.0 & 0.0 \\
26	1.0 mm  & 100.0 & 0.0 & 0.0 \\
27	10 mm   & 100.0 & 0.0 & 0.0 \\
28	\bottomrule
29	\end{tabular}
30	\end{table}
31	
32	The choice of $\tau_d = 0.1$\,mm represents a conservative threshold: displacement below this value is physically negligible in manipulation tasks where typical action magnitudes are 1--10\,mm per step. Even at $\tau_d = 1$\,$\mu$m, 73.9\% of A1-LoRA safe episodes are classified as frozen, while all Pi0.5 episodes remain above threshold at every level tested.
33	
34	Table~\ref{tab:dfcar_threshold} extends this analysis to dfCAR across fine-tuned Pi0.5 configurations. All eight model-seed combinations yield identical dfCAR for $\tau_d \in [2, 200]$\,mm. At $\tau_d = 300$\,mm, 0--2 episodes per seed fall below threshold (displacement 200--300\,mm), causing $\leq$0.5\,pp deviation. Our conclusions hold across the entire tested range.
35	
36	\begin{table}[h]
37	\centering
38	\caption{dfCAR (\%) across displacement thresholds for fine-tuned Pi0.5 configurations ($n = 200$ episodes per seed). Values are identical across $\tau_d \in [2, 200]$\,mm; at $\tau_d = 300$\,mm, at most 2 episodes are excluded.}
39	\label{tab:dfcar_threshold}
40	\vspace{2pt}
41	\footnotesize
42	\begin{tabular}{lcccccccc}
43	\toprule
44	& \multicolumn{8}{c}{$\tau_d$ (mm)} \\
45	\cmidrule(lr){2-9}
46	Condition & 2 & 5 & 10 & 20 & 50 & 100 & 200 & 300 \\
47	\midrule
48	LoRA s0            & 57.0 & 57.0 & 57.0 & 57.0 & 57.0 & 57.0 & 57.0 & 57.0 \\
49	LoRA s1            & 52.5 & 52.5 & 52.5 & 52.5 & 52.5 & 52.5 & 52.5 & 52.5 \\
50	SDF $\lambda{=}0.005$ s0 & 57.0 & 57.0 & 57.0 & 57.0 & 57.0 & 57.0 & 57.0 & 57.1 \\
51	SDF $\lambda{=}0.005$ s1 & 54.0 & 54.0 & 54.0 & 54.0 & 54.0 & 54.0 & 54.0 & 54.0 \\
52	SDF $\lambda{=}0.005$ s2 & 52.5 & 52.5 & 52.5 & 52.5 & 52.5 & 52.5 & 52.5 & 52.3 \\

139	\section{Per-Task Oracle Analysis}
140	\label{sec:appendix_pertask}
141	\label{sec:appendix_pertask_oracle}
142	
143	Table~\ref{tab:oracle_pertask} shows per-task CAR for oracle refinement. On task~1 (\texttt{chocolate\_pudding}), where the EE is the primary collision body and the LoRA baseline achieves only 46.0\% CAR, Oracle-adaptive improves to 55.0\% and Oracle-feature to 47.5\%. On the remaining tasks, all oracle variants degrade: Oracle-adaptive reduces task~0 (\texttt{orange\_juice}) from 96.0\% to 82.5\% and task~2 (\texttt{milk}) from 98.0\% to 77.5\%. The SDF gradient pushes the EE away from the nearest surface, but the resulting joint-space displacement rotates intermediate arm links into obstacle volumes.
144	
145	The feature-space oracle fires on nearly every timestep (241.7 refinements/episode vs.\ 14.2 for Cartesian variants) but with gradient magnitudes two orders of magnitude smaller (0.004--0.006 vs.\ 0.18--0.49), and CAR (76.9\%) shows no advantage despite 17$\times$ more steps. The A3 learned SDF never triggers refinement (0 steps/episode), as its outputs avoid the activation threshold.

167	\section{Null Distribution for SDF Loss Weight Analysis}
168	\label{sec:appendix_null}
169	
170	We construct a null distribution by pooling the three LoRA-only seeds and three $\lambda = 0.005$ seeds into six per-seed CAR measurements: 57.0\% (LoRA s0, re-evaluated), 52.5\% (LoRA s1), 55.0\% (LoRA s2), 57.0\% ($\lambda{=}0.005$ s0), 54.0\% ($\lambda{=}0.005$ s1), 52.5\% ($\lambda{=}0.005$ s2), yielding mean 54.67\% and standard deviation 1.94\%. We treat these as exchangeable because SDF at $\lambda = 0.005$ produces no separation from LoRA-only. The $\lambda = 0.01$ mean of 56.33\% falls within $0.9\sigma$ of this null ($p > 0.1$), consistent with seed-to-seed variation rather than a treatment effect. The $\lambda = 0.1$ 30K mean of 58.25\% (2 seeds) yields $z = 1.85$ ($p \approx 0.032$), a marginal departure whose interpretation is limited by $n = 2$.
171	
172	A Phase~2 ablation reinforces this conclusion. Setting $\lambda = 0$ during Phase~2 (steps 5K--30K), so that SDF supervision is active only during auxiliary head warmup (Phase~1, steps 0--5K), yields CAR of 57.5\% (single seed, s2). This value lies within the null range, indicating that SDF training produces no lasting behavioral effect on collision avoidance.
173

The seed-0 curve shows no monotonic trend through 28K; the 30K checkpoint marks the first consistent dual-seed departure from the null band, though whether this reflects a genuine late-training effect or sampling variance at $n = 2$ remains open.

This is consistent with binomial sampling noise at $n = 50$ episodes per task ($\sigma \approx 7$\,pp per task for $p \approx 0.5$). The seed-averaged curve shows no monotonic trend, confirming that SDF loss weight does not produce a progressive training signal.
201	
202	
203	\section{Oracle SDF Refinement Details}
204	\label{sec:appendix_oracle}
205	
206	All oracle variants replace the learned SDF decoder with ground-truth distance fields computed from the simulation geometry. Each is evaluated for 160 episodes (4 tasks $\times$ 40).
207	
208	\paragraph{Oracle-adaptive.} At each timestep, if the ground-truth SDF at the end-effector position falls below a safety margin, the action is adjusted by a step in the negative SDF gradient direction. The step size $\alpha$ is scaled inversely with distance: smaller distances produce larger corrections. This mimics an adaptive potential field controller.
209	
210	\paragraph{Oracle-fixed.} Identical to Oracle-adaptive except the step size is fixed at $\alpha = 0.005$ regardless of distance. This isolates the effect of step-size adaptation from the refinement geometry.
211	
212	\paragraph{Oracle-feature.} Instead of modifying the action directly in Cartesian space, this variant applies the SDF gradient in the policy's latent feature space via backpropagation. The gradient of the ground-truth SDF value with respect to the intermediate features is computed and used to perturb the features before the action head decodes them. This tests whether geometric corrections are more effective when applied in the model's learned representation space. In practice, the feature-space variant fires on nearly every timestep (241.7 refinements per episode vs.\ 14.2 for Cartesian variants) but with gradient magnitudes two orders of magnitude smaller (0.004--0.006 vs.\ 0.18--0.49 in action space).
213	
214	
215	\section{Training Details}
216	\label{sec:appendix_training}
217	
218	\paragraph{Architecture.}
219	We use LoRA~\citep{hu2022lora} adapters injected into the PaliGemma vision encoder (rank 16, $\alpha$=16) and the action expert (rank 32, $\alpha$=32), following the configuration of OpenPi. The SDF decoder is a 4-layer MLP (hidden dimension 256) with skip connections, taking pooled suffix features (1024-d) and query points (3-d) as input. The end-effector extractor is a 2-layer MLP (1024 $\to$ 256 $\to$ 3).
220	
221	\paragraph{Training schedule.}
222	Training proceeds in two phases:
223	\begin{itemize}
224	    \item \textbf{Phase 1} (steps 0--5000): Backbone and LoRA parameters are frozen. Only the SDF decoder and EE extractor are trained, allowing them to learn meaningful representations before the policy adapts.
225	    \item \textbf{Phase 2} (steps 5000--30000): LoRA parameters are unfrozen with a 100-step gradient warmup ramp. All auxiliary heads continue training jointly with the policy.
226	\end{itemize}
227	
228	\paragraph{Hyperparameters.}
229	\begin{table}[h]
230	\centering
231	\caption{Training hyperparameters.}
232	\label{tab:hyperparams}
233	\begin{tabular}{ll}
234	\toprule
235	Parameter & Value \\
236	\midrule
237	Base model & Pi0.5 (OpenPi, $\sim$3.5B parameters) \\
238	Optimizer & AdamW \\
239	Learning rate & $2.5 \times 10^{-5}$ (cosine decay to $\mathrm{lr}/10$) \\
240	Warmup steps & 1000 \\
241	Batch size & 32 \\
242	Total steps & 30000 \\
243	$\lambda_\mathrm{sdf}$ & \{0.005, 0.01, 0.1\} \\
244	$\lambda_\mathrm{ee}$ & 0.01 \\
245	SDF query points per step & 64 \\
246	SDF clamp $\delta$ & 0.05 m \\
247	Eikonal weight & 0.1 \\
248	LoRA rank (vision) & 16 \\
249	LoRA rank (action) & 32 \\
250	Phase 1 duration & 5000 steps \\
251	\bottomrule
252	\end{tabular}
253	\end{table}
254	
255	\paragraph{Evaluation protocol.}
256	Each episode runs for at most 300 timesteps. The policy generates a 50-step action trajectory via flow matching and executes the first 5 steps before replanning. An episode terminates early upon task completion. Collision detection uses MuJoCo's built-in contact detection: any contact between the robot and a non-target object registers as a collision. For Pi0.5 evaluations, camera images are rotated 180$^\circ$ to match the pre-training viewpoint convention; this correction was verified to restore TSR from ${\sim}$5\% to ${\sim}$52\% while leaving CAR comparable. Oracle variants use 40 episodes per task (160 total) with a single seed; all other configurations use 50 episodes per task (200 total).
257	
258	\paragraph{Data.}
259	We use the SafeLIBERO object\_pudding suite with pre-computed SDF ground truth. SDF values are computed offline from the obstacle mesh using a dense $64^3$ voxel grid. During training, 64 query points are randomly sampled per step from this grid.
260	
261	\paragraph{Computational resources.}
262	All training and evaluation experiments are conducted on NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 GPUs (96\,GB). Phase~1 (5K steps) takes approximately 6 hours on a single GPU; Phase~2 (25K steps) takes approximately 32 hours. Evaluation of 200 episodes requires approximately 4 hours. Across 100+ experiments (training runs, evaluations, oracle variants, and ablations), the total computational budget is approximately 700 GPU-hours.
263

The SDF gradient pushes the EE away from the nearest surface, but the resulting joint-space displacement rotates intermediate arm links into obstacle volumes.\n\nThe feature-space oracle fires on nearly every timestep (241.7 refinements/episode vs.\\ 14.2 for Cartesian variants) but with gradient magnitudes two orders of magnitude smaller (0.004--0.006 vs.\\ 0.18--0.49), and CAR (76.9\\%) shows no advantage despite 17$\\times$ more steps. The A3 learned SDF never triggers refinement (0 steps/episode), as its outputs avoid the activation threshold.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Per-task CAR (\\%) for oracle SDF refinement variants. EE-only refinement helps only on task~1 where the EE is the primary collision body; on other tasks it trades one collision site for another.}\n\\label{tab:oracle_pertask}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & Task 0 & Task 1 & Task 2 & Task 3 & Agg. \\\\\n\\midrule\nLoRA baseline & 96.0 & 46.0 & 98.0 & 88.0 & 82.0 \\\\\n\\midrule\nOracle-adaptive & 82.5 & 55.0 & 77.5 & 85.0 & 75.0 \\\\\nOracle-fixed & 85.0 & 50.0 & 77.5 & 80.0 & 73.1 \\\\\nOracle-feature & 90.0 & 47.5 & 82.5 & 87.5 & 76.9 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\section{Null Distribution for SDF Loss Weight Analysis}\n\\label{sec:appendix_null}\n\nWe construct a null distribution by pooling the three LoRA-only seeds and three $\\lambda = 0.005$ seeds into six per-seed CAR measurements: 57.0\\% (LoRA s0, re-evaluated), 52.5\\% (LoRA s1), 55.0\\% (LoRA s2), 57.0\\% ($\\lambda{=}0.005$ s0), 54.0\\% ($\\lambda{=}0.005$ s1), 52.5\\% ($\\lambda{=}0.005$ s2), yielding mean 54.67\\% and standard deviation 1.94\\%. We treat these as exchangeable because SDF at $\\lambda = 0.005$ produces no separation from LoRA-only. The $\\lambda = 0.01$ mean of 56.33\\% falls within $0.9\\sigma$ of this null ($p > 0.1$), consistent with seed-to-seed variation rather than a treatment effect. The $\\lambda = 0.1$ 30K mean of 58.25\\% (2 seeds) yields $z = 1.85$ ($p \\approx 0.032$), a marginal departure whose interpretation is limited by $n = 2$.\n\nA Phase~2 ablation reinforces this conclusion. Setting $\\lambda = 0$ during Phase~2 (steps 5K--30K), so that SDF supervision is active only during auxiliary head warmup (Phase~1, steps 0--5K), yields CAR of 57.5\\% (single seed, s2). This value lies within the null range, indicating that SDF training produces no lasting behavioral effect on collision avoidance.\n\n\\section{Step-wise Checkpoint Analysis for \\texorpdfstring{$\\lambda = 0.1$}{lambda = 0.1}}\n\\label{sec:appendix_stepwise}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:stepwise_lambda01} reports per-seed CAR at seven checkpoints for $\\lambda = 0.1$. Seed-1 data is available at 16K, 20K, 22K, and 30K; 24K--28K report seed-0 only. The seed-0 curve shows no monotonic trend through 28K; the 30K checkpoint marks the first consistent dual-seed departure from the null band, though whether this reflects a genuine late-training effect or sampling variance at $n = 2$ remains open.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Per-seed CAR (\\%) at training checkpoints for $\\lambda = 0.1$ (200 episodes each). 24K--28K: seed-0 only. Dual-seed means remain within the null band (54.67\\%\\,$\\pm$\\,1.94\\%) at all checkpoints except 30K, where both seeds depart marginally ($z = 1.85$, $p \\approx 0.032$).}\n\\label{tab:stepwise_lambda01}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nCheckpoint & Seed 0 & Seed 1 & Mean \\\\\n\\midrule\n16K steps & 55.5 & 56.0 & 55.75 \\\\\n20K steps & 55.0 & 56.0 & 55.5 \\\\\n22K steps & 60.5 & 56.5 & 58.5 \\\\\n24K steps & 52.5 & -- & -- \\\\\n26K steps & 53.5 & -- & -- \\\\\n28K steps & 54.5 & -- & -- \\\\\n30K steps & 58.0 & 58.5 & 58.25 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nIndividual seeds exhibit checkpoint-to-checkpoint CAR fluctuations of up to 8\\,pp (seed~0: 52.5--60.5\\%), larger than the cross-seed standard deviation at any fixed checkpoint ($\\leq$2.8\\,pp). This is consistent with binomial sampling noise at $n = 50$ episodes per task ($\\sigma \\approx 7$\\,pp per task for $p \\approx 0.5$). The seed-averaged curve shows no monotonic trend, confirming that SDF loss weight does not produce a progressive training signal.\n\n\n\\section{Oracle SDF Refinement Details}\n\\label{sec:appendix_oracle}\n\nAll oracle variants replace the learned SDF decoder with ground-truth distance fields computed from the simulation geometry. Each is evaluated for 160 episodes (4 tasks $\\times$ 40).\n\n\\paragraph{Oracle-adaptive.} At each timestep, if the ground-truth SDF at the end-effector position falls below a safety margin, the action is adjusted by a step in the negative SDF gradient direction. The step size $\\alpha$ is scaled inversely with distance: smaller distances produce larger corrections. This mimics an adaptive potential field controller.\n\n\\paragraph{Oracle-fixed.} Identical to Oracle-adaptive except the step size is fixed at $\\alpha = 0.005$ regardless of distance. This isolates the effect of step-size adaptation from the refinement geometry.\n\n\\paragraph{Oracle-feature.} Instead of modifying the action directly in Cartesian space, this variant applies the SDF gradient in the policy's latent feature space via backpropagation. The gradient of the ground-truth SDF value with respect to the intermediate features is computed and used to perturb the features before the action head decodes them. This tests whether geometric corrections are more effective when applied in the model's learned representation space. In practice, the feature-space variant fires on nearly every timestep (241.7 refinements per episode vs.\\ 14.2 for Cartesian variants) but with gradient magnitudes two orders of magnitude smaller (0.004--0.006 vs.\\ 0.18--0.49 in action space).\n\n\n\\section{Training Details}\n\\label{sec:appendix_training}\n\n\\paragraph{Architecture.}\nWe use LoRA~\\citep{hu2022lora} adapters injected into the PaliGemma vision encoder (rank 16, $\\alpha$=16) and the action expert (rank 32, $\\alpha$=32), following the configuration of OpenPi. The SDF decoder is a 4-layer MLP (hidden dimension 256) with skip connections, taking pooled suffix features (1024-d) and query points (3-d) as input. The end-effector extractor is a 2-layer MLP (1024 $\\to$ 256 $\\to$ 3).\n\n\\paragraph{Training schedule.}\nTraining proceeds in two phases:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Phase 1} (steps 0--5000): Backbone and LoRA parameters are frozen. Only the SDF decoder and EE extractor are trained, allowing them to learn meaningful representations before the policy adapts.\n    \\item \\textbf{Phase 2} (steps 5000--30000): LoRA parameters are unfrozen with a 100-step gradient warmup ramp. All auxiliary heads continue training jointly with the policy.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\paragraph{Hyperparameters.}\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Training hyperparameters.}\n\\label{tab:hyperparams}\n\\begin{tabular}{ll}\n\\toprule\nParameter & Value \\\\\n\\midrule\nBase model & Pi0.5 (OpenPi, $\\sim$3.5B parameters) \\\\\nOptimizer & AdamW \\\\\nLearning rate & $2.5 \\times 10^{-5}$ (cosine decay to $\\mathrm{lr}/10$) \\\\\nWarmup steps & 1000 \\\\\nBatch size & 32 \\\\\nTotal steps & 30000 \\\\\n$\\lambda_\\mathrm{sdf}$ & \\{0.005, 0.01, 0.1\\} \\\\\n$\\lambda_\\mathrm{ee}$ & 0.01 \\\\\nSDF query points per step & 64 \\\\\nSDF clamp $\\delta$ & 0.05 m \\\\\nEikonal weight & 0.1 \\\\\nLoRA rank (vision) & 16 \\\\\nLoRA rank (action) & 32 \\\\\nPhase 1 duration & 5000 steps \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Evaluation protocol.}\nEach episode runs for at most 300 timesteps. The policy generates a 50-step action trajectory via flow matching and executes the first 5 steps before replanning. An episode terminates early upon task completion. Collision detection uses MuJoCo's built-in contact detection: any contact between the robot and a non-target object registers as a collision. For Pi0.5 evaluations, camera images are rotated 180$^\\circ$ to match the pre-training viewpoint convention; this correction was verified to restore TSR from ${\\sim}$5\\% to ${\\sim}$52\\% while leaving CAR comparable. Oracle variants use 40 episodes per task (160 total) with a single seed; all other configurations use 50 episodes per task (200 total).\n\n\\paragraph{Data.}\nWe use the SafeLIBERO object\\_pudding suite with pre-computed SDF ground truth. SDF values are computed offline from the obstacle mesh using a dense $64^3$ voxel grid. During training, 64 query points are randomly sampled per step from this grid.\n\n\\paragraph{Computational resources.}\nAll training and evaluation experiments are conducted on NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 GPUs (96\\,GB). Phase~1 (5K steps) takes approximately 6 hours on a single GPU; Phase~2 (25K steps) takes approximately 32 hours. Evaluation of 200 episodes requires approximately 4 hours. Across 100+ experiments (training runs, evaluations, oracle variants, and ablations), the total computational budget is approximately 700 GPU-hours.\n",
      "numLines": 263,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 263
    }
  }
}

    "originalFile": "\\section{Avoidance Source Diagnosis}\\label{sec:avoidance_source}\n\nFine-tuning on collision-free demonstrations produces genuine collision avoidance: LoRA-adapted Pi0.5 raises CAR by ${\\sim}$28 percentage points with zero frozen episodes. Does SDF geometric supervision contribute additional avoidance beyond what demonstrations provide? We compare LoRA-only, LoRA+SDF ($\\lambda = 0.005$), and LoRA+SDF ($\\lambda = 0.01$) on Pi0.5, all evaluated at the 30K-step checkpoint.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Avoidance source attribution on SafeLIBERO. SDF at $\\lambda \\leq 0.01$ adds no measurable CAR beyond LoRA-only ($p > 0.1$); $\\lambda = 0.1$ shows a marginal signal ($z = 1.85$, $p \\approx 0.032$, $n{=}2$) that requires additional seeds. All fine-tuned models exhibit 0\\% frozen rate and displacement $>$\\,0.37\\,m.}\n\\label{tab:avoidance_source}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & Seeds & TSR (\\%) & CAR (\\%) & EE Disp (m) \\\\\n\\midrule\nPi0.5 baseline & 3 & 74.7\\,$\\pm$\\,1.5 & 26.8\\,$\\pm$\\,1.6 & 0.367 \\\\\n\\midrule\nLoRA-only (no SDF) & 3 & 49.7\\,$\\pm$\\,3.2 & 54.83\\,$\\pm$\\,2.25 & 0.391 \\\\\nLoRA+SDF $\\lambda{=}0.005$ & 3 & 54.0\\,$\\pm$\\,3.0 & 54.5\\,$\\pm$\\,2.29 & 0.387 \\\\\nLoRA+SDF $\\lambda{=}0.01$ & 3 & 53.8\\,$\\pm$\\,1.5 & 56.33\\,$\\pm$\\,1.61 & 0.389 \\\\\nLoRA+SDF $\\lambda{=}0.1$\\textsuperscript{$\\dagger$} & 2 & 48.0\\,$\\pm$\\,1.4 & 58.25\\,$\\pm$\\,0.35 & 0.394 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\vspace{1pt}\n{\\footnotesize \\textsuperscript{$\\dagger$}30K checkpoint, two of three seeds; third seed in progress.}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:avoidance_source} shows that collision-free demonstrations alone raise CAR by ${\\sim}$28 percentage points (26.8\\% $\\to$ 54.83\\%) with zero frozen episodes and displacement above 0.37\\,m, confirming genuine avoidance. Adding SDF supervision produces no measurable benefit: LoRA-only CAR (54.83\\%) is within 0.25\\,pp of $\\lambda = 0.005$ and statistically indistinguishable from $\\lambda = 0.01$ (56.33\\%, $p > 0.1$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_null}). A Phase~2 ablation ($\\lambda = 0$ after step 5K) yields CAR\\,=\\,57.5\\%\\footnote{Single seed (s2); computational constraints precluded additional seeds for this ablation.}, confirming that SDF gradients produce no lasting behavioral effect. The per-task CAR ordering is preserved across all conditions (chocolate\\_pudding highest, bbq\\_sauce lowest) and invariant to $\\lambda$ across a 20$\\times$ range, indicating that avoidance patterns reflect the demonstration distribution rather than geometric supervision.\n\n\\paragraph{CAR--TSR trade-off.}\\label{sec:tsr_collapse}\nFine-tuning degrades TSR by ${\\sim}$21--25\\,pp (74.7\\% $\\to$ 49.7--54.0\\%), reflecting distributional shift from the pre-trained prior rather than learned paralysis (all models retain $>$\\,0.37\\,m displacement and 0\\% frozen episodes). The SSR/TSR ratio increases from 0.33 (baseline) to 0.51--0.57 (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_ssr}), confirming that among task-completing episodes, a larger fraction avoids collisions after fine-tuning.\n\n\\paragraph{Dose-response at higher loss weights.}\\label{sec:lambda_high}\nIncreasing $\\lambda$ by 20$\\times$ to 0.1 yields CAR\\,=\\,58.25\\%\\,$\\pm$\\,0.35 at the 30K checkpoint (two of three seeds), above the null distribution (mean 54.67\\%, $\\sigma = 1.94$, $z = 1.85$, $p \\approx 0.032$). This departure is suggestive but inconclusive at $n = 2$. A stepwise ablation across six checkpoints (16K--28K) shows no sustained trend (mean 55.3\\%, within 0.3$\\sigma$ of the null; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_stepwise}), and per-task CAR ordering at 30K remains identical to all other conditions (Figure~\\ref{fig:lambda_car}). The response is thus dose-dependent: SDF is fully redundant at $\\lambda \\leq 0.01$ ($p > 0.1$), with a marginal signal at $\\lambda = 0.1$ whose modest effect size (${\\sim}$3.6\\,pp) and limited sample preclude strong conclusions. Even at elevated $\\lambda$, avoidance patterns remain driven by demonstration distribution rather than geometric supervision.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.85\\linewidth]{fig5_lambda_curve}\n\\caption{Effect of SDF loss weight $\\lambda$ on CAR and TSR. $\\lambda = 0$: LoRA-only. Low $\\lambda$ ($\\leq 0.01$) remains within the null band; $\\lambda = 0.1$ (58.25\\%, 2 seeds) shows a marginal departure ($p \\approx 0.032$) whose per-task pattern still matches the demonstration distribution.}\n\\label{fig:lambda_car}\n\\end{figure}",

    "newString": "$p{\\approx}0.039$)",
    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nVision-Language-Action (VLA) models report high Collision Avoidance Rates (CAR) on safety benchmarks, yet we show these metrics are contaminated by action collapse.\nOn SafeLIBERO, an SDF-augmented Pi0 achieves 91.5\\% CAR while 99.6\\% of its safe episodes exhibit end-effector displacement below 0.1\\,mm---the robot is frozen, not safe.\nWe present displacement-filtered CAR (dfCAR), which excludes sub-threshold episodes, as a corrective metric; the bimodal displacement distribution makes it robust across a $[2, 200]$\\,mm threshold range.\nOur analysis further reveals that end-effector-only Signed Distance Field (SDF) supervision is neither necessary nor structurally sufficient for collision avoidance.\nIt is counterproductive at inference time: three ground-truth oracle variants all reduce dfCAR below unrefined baselines, as SDF gradients repelling the end-effector drive arm links into obstacles.\nIts contribution during training is dose-dependent: LoRA-only fine-tuning raises dfCAR from 26.8\\% to 54.83\\%$\\,\\pm\\,$2.25, matching SDF-augmented training at $\\lambda{=}0.005$ (54.5\\%$\\,\\pm\\,$2.29) and $\\lambda{=}0.01$ (56.33\\%$\\,\\pm\\,$1.61); at $\\lambda{=}0.1$, two of three seeds show a marginal signal (58.25\\%$\\,\\pm\\,$0.35, $p{\\approx}0.032$) whose per-task avoidance pattern still mirrors the demonstration distribution, requiring further investigation.\nPer-task avoidance patterns remain identical across all conditions, indicating that collision avoidance arises from collision-free demonstration data rather than geometric supervision.\nDisplacement filtering is necessary for meaningful VLA safety evaluation; EE-only geometric supervision can be neither credited with nor expected to deliver collision avoidance.\n\\end{abstract}",

        "oldStart": 4,
        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 4,

    "newString": "($p \\approx 0.039$, $n = 2$)",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:intro}\n\nVision-Language-Action (VLA) models must avoid collisions to operate safely in physical environments~\\cite{li2026vlasafety}. The SafeLIBERO benchmark~\\cite{hu2025vlsa} provides the first systematic protocol for measuring collision avoidance in VLA manipulation, and recent methods, including SDF-augmented training~\\cite{zhang2025safevla}, constraint-based planning~\\cite{yang2025cape}, and collision-free demonstrations~\\cite{cofreeevla2026}, report high Collision Avoidance Rate (CAR). Our SDF-augmented Pi0~\\cite{black2024pi0} reaches 91.5\\% CAR across 800 episodes. The problem appears close to solved.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{fig1_teaser}\n\\caption{The fake safety problem in VLA collision avoidance evaluation. Standard CAR classifies a frozen robot as safe because it produces zero collisions (left). dfCAR filters out action-collapsed episodes where displacement falls below $\\tau_d$, revealing that Pi0+A1's 91.5\\% safety score drops to ${\\approx}$\\,0\\% (center). Pi0.5 exhibits genuine avoidance with active motion above 288\\,mm in all safe episodes, and dfCAR equals raw CAR (right).}\n\\label{fig:teaser}\n\\end{figure}\n\nIt is not (Figure~\\ref{fig:teaser}). Of the 732 collision-free episodes, 99.6\\% exhibit displacement below 0.1\\,mm; TSR is 0\\% across all 800 episodes. The robot is frozen, not safe. The same pattern holds across all Pi0 configurations (Section~\\ref{sec:fake_safety}). We term this \\emph{fake safety}: \\emph{action collapse} (near-zero actions producing zero collisions) renders reported CAR illusory, and standard evaluation cannot distinguish inaction from deliberate avoidance~\\cite{karwowski2024goodhart}.\n\nWe present a systematic investigation of this problem across the Pi0 and Pi0.5~\\cite{black2024pi0} model families on SafeLIBERO, spanning 100+ experiments and 6000+ evaluation episodes. Three findings emerge.\n\n\\textbf{Fake safety is pervasive.} We introduce displacement-filtered CAR (dfCAR), which excludes episodes below a displacement threshold $\\tau_d$. dfCAR collapses Pi0+A1 from 91.5\\% to ${\\approx}$\\,0\\% (3/732 safe episodes survive). The bimodal displacement distribution makes $\\tau_d$ robust across five orders of magnitude. Pi0.5 validates the metric: TSR\\,=\\,74.7\\%, CAR\\,=\\,26.8\\%, with every safe episode above 288\\,mm displacement and dfCAR equal to raw CAR (Section~\\ref{sec:fake_safety}).\n\n\\textbf{EE-only SDF refinement is structurally counterproductive.} Three ground-truth oracle variants all achieve \\emph{lower} CAR than unrefined baselines (73.1--76.9\\% vs.\\ 82--83\\%). The SDF gradient repels the EE but rotates arm links into obstacle volumes (Section~\\ref{sec:ee_only}).\n\n\\textbf{Collision-free demonstrations are sufficient for collision avoidance.} LoRA fine-tuning on Pi0.5 raises CAR by ${\\sim}$28 points (26.8\\% $\\to$ 54.83\\%) with zero frozen episodes; adding SDF supervision at low loss weights ($\\lambda \\in \\{0.005, 0.01\\}$) produces no measurable benefit and at high $\\lambda = 0.1$ yields only a marginal, inconclusive signal ($p \\approx 0.032$, $n = 2$); the per-task avoidance pattern is identical with and without geometric supervision, indicating that the collision-free demonstration distribution, not EE-only SDF supervision, accounts for all observed avoidance learning on this benchmark (Section~\\ref{sec:avoidance_source}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{dfCAR metric:} displacement-filtered CAR that separates genuine avoidance from action collapse, robust across $\\tau_d \\in [2, 200]$\\,mm.\n    \\item \\textbf{Fake safety diagnosis:} 100+ experiments across Pi0/Pi0.5 showing $>$99\\% of Pi0 safe episodes are action-collapse artifacts.\n    \\item \\textbf{EE-only SDF is counterproductive:} ground-truth oracle refinement \\emph{reduces} CAR below unrefined baselines by redistributing collisions to unmonitored arm links.\n    \\item \\textbf{Demonstration sufficiency:} on SafeLIBERO, collision-free demonstrations account for all measured CAR gains (${\\sim}$28\\,pp); EE-only SDF at low $\\lambda$ ($\\leq 0.01$) adds nothing, and at high $\\lambda = 0.1$ shows only a marginal, inconclusive signal ($n = 2$) whose per-task pattern still matches the demonstration distribution.\n\\end{itemize}",

The SDF gradient repels the EE but rotates arm links into obstacle volumes (Section~\\ref{sec:ee_only}).",
          " ",
          "-\\textbf{Collision-free demonstrations are sufficient for collision avoidance.} LoRA fine-tuning on Pi0.5 raises CAR by ${\\sim}$28 points (26.8\\% $\\to$ 54.83\\%) with zero frozen episodes; adding SDF supervision at low loss weights ($\\lambda \\in \\{0.005, 0.01\\}$) produces no measurable benefit and at high $\\lambda = 0.1$ yields only a marginal, inconclusive signal ($p \\approx 0.032$, $n = 2$); the per-task avoidance pattern is identical with and without geometric supervision, indicating that the collision-free demonstration distribution, not EE-only SDF supervision, accounts for all observed avoidance learning on this benchmark (Section~\\ref{sec:avoidance_source}).",
          "+\\textbf{Collision-free demonstrations are sufficient for collision avoidance.} LoRA fine-tuning on Pi0.5 raises CAR by ${\\sim}$28 points (26.8\\% $\\to$ 54.83\\%) with zero frozen episodes; adding SDF supervision at low loss weights ($\\lambda \\in \\{0.005, 0.01\\}$) produces no measurable benefit and at high $\\lambda = 0.1$ yields only a marginal, inconclusive signal ($p \\approx 0.039$, $n = 2$); the per-task avoidance pattern is identical with and without geometric supervision, indicating that the collision-free demonstration distribution, not EE-only SDF supervision, accounts for all observed avoidance learning on this benchmark (Section~\\ref{sec:avoidance_source}).",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion and Conclusion}\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nAcross 100+ experiments and 6000+ evaluation episodes on two VLA model families, three findings converge: current VLA safety evaluation is structurally unreliable. Action collapse renders reported CAR illusory (\\S\\ref{sec:dfcar}); EE-only oracle refinement is counterproductive (\\S\\ref{sec:ee_only}); and collision-free demonstrations, not geometric supervision, account for all measured avoidance gains (\\S\\ref{sec:avoidance_source}).\n\n\\paragraph{Why EE-only SDF fails.}\nTwo independent causes explain the null result. \\textit{(i)~Demonstration data subsumes the SDF signal.} Collision-free demonstrations implicitly encode spatial avoidance; the ${\\sim}$28\\,pp CAR gain arises entirely from this distributional shift, with per-task ordering invariant across all SDF conditions (\\S\\ref{sec:avoidance_source}). At low $\\lambda$, SDF is redundant; at $\\lambda = 0.1$, the marginal signal preserves the same demonstration-driven pattern. \\textit{(ii)~EE monitoring cannot prevent full-body collisions.} The SDF gradient acts on a single point while collisions occur along intermediate links. Oracle experiments confirm this: ground-truth SDF refinement \\emph{reduces} CAR by 6--10\\,pp below unrefined baselines (\\S\\ref{sec:ee_only}), because pushing the EE away rotates unmonitored links through obstacles. These levels are independent; effective geometric safety requires both full-body supervision and training signals beyond demonstrations.\n\n\\paragraph{Implications for evaluation.}\ndfCAR corrects action-collapse contamination using only EE position logging, already available in most evaluation pipelines. The bimodal displacement distribution makes $\\tau_d$ robust across five orders of magnitude. We recommend reporting both CAR and dfCAR; the gap quantifies contamination. This contamination spans a 4000$\\times$ parameter range (Pi0 Full-FT ${\\sim}$2.5B to Pi0+A1 ${\\sim}$658K trainable), confirming it is a structural consequence of evaluating avoidance without verifying motion. More broadly, safety may need to operate at the planning or joint-space constraint layer, since any EE-centric loss inherits the same blind spot.\n\n\\paragraph{Limitations.}\nOur evaluation covers one benchmark suite (SafeLIBERO, four tasks) and two model families (Pi0, Pi0.5). The tabletop setting with fixed-base manipulator and known obstacles may not generalize to mobile platforms and cluttered scenes. Oracle experiments used a largely frozen model (Pi0+A1, TSR${\\approx}$0\\%); testing on an actively moving policy would better isolate the structural limitation. The $\\lambda = 0.1$ condition has $n = 2$ seeds; the marginal departure ($p \\approx 0.032$) is inconclusive. Full-body SDF remains untested. Fine-tuning degrades TSR by ${\\sim}$21--25\\,pp, yet SSR/TSR increases from 0.33 to 0.51--0.57 (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_ssr}), ruling out reduced exposure as the sole explanation.\n\n\\paragraph{Future work.}\nThree directions follow: (1) full-body SDF supervision along the kinematic chain; (2) cross-architecture evaluation on OpenVLA, RT-2, and other VLA families; (3) planning-layer safety constraints in joint space rather than EE space.\n\n\\paragraph{Broader impact.}\nContaminated CAR scores allow models with no genuine avoidance to pass safety benchmarks. dfCAR exposes this gap with minimal changes to existing protocols.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Displacement Distribution}\n\\label{sec:appendix_displacement}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{fig2_ee_displacement_distribution}\n\\caption{Displacement distribution across evaluation episodes. Pi0-family models (blue) cluster below 1\\,$\\mu$m, while Pi0.5 episodes (orange) exceed 200\\,mm. The five-order-of-magnitude gap makes $\\tau_d$ robust to any value in $[2, 200]$\\,mm.}\n\\label{fig:displacement}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\section{Displacement Threshold Sensitivity Analysis}\n\\label{sec:appendix_threshold}\n\nTo validate the robustness of our displacement threshold $\\tau_d$, we evaluate the frozen rate across four orders of magnitude. Table~\\ref{tab:threshold_sensitivity} shows that the fake safety pattern persists across all reasonable threshold choices: A1-LoRA exhibits near-total action collapse regardless of threshold, while Pi0.5+A1 maintains 0\\% frozen rate even at the most conservative setting.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Frozen rate (\\% of episodes with $d_\\mathrm{max} < \\tau_d$) across threshold values.}\n\\label{tab:threshold_sensitivity}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n$\\tau_d$ & A1-LoRA (Pi0) & Pi0.5 Baseline & Pi0.5+A1 (SDF aux) \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.001 mm (1\\,$\\mu$m) & 73.9 & 0.0 & 0.0 \\\\\n0.1 mm  & 99.6 & 0.0 & 0.0 \\\\\n1.0 mm  & 100.0 & 0.0 & 0.0 \\\\\n10 mm   & 100.0 & 0.0 & 0.0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe choice of $\\tau_d = 0.1$\\,mm represents a conservative threshold: displacement below this value is physically negligible in manipulation tasks where typical action magnitudes are 1--10\\,mm per step. Even at $\\tau_d = 1$\\,$\\mu$m, 73.9\\% of A1-LoRA safe episodes are classified as frozen, while all Pi0.5 episodes remain above threshold at every level tested.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:dfcar_threshold} extends this analysis to dfCAR across fine-tuned Pi0.5 configurations. All eight model-seed combinations yield identical dfCAR for $\\tau_d \\in [2, 200]$\\,mm. At $\\tau_d = 300$\\,mm, 0--2 episodes per seed fall below threshold (displacement 200--300\\,mm), causing $\\leq$0.5\\,pp deviation. Our conclusions hold across the entire tested range.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{dfCAR (\\%) across displacement thresholds for fine-tuned Pi0.5 configurations ($n = 200$ episodes per seed). Values are identical across $\\tau_d \\in [2, 200]$\\,mm; at $\\tau_d = 300$\\,mm, at most 2 episodes are excluded.}\n\\label{tab:dfcar_threshold}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccccccc}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{8}{c}{$\\tau_d$ (mm)} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-9}\nCondition & 2 & 5 & 10 & 20 & 50 & 100 & 200 & 300 \\\\\n\\midrule\nLoRA s0            & 57.0 & 57.0 & 57.0 & 57.0 & 57.0 & 57.0 & 57.0 & 57.0 \\\\\nLoRA s1            & 52.5 & 52.5 & 52.5 & 52.5 & 52.5 & 52.5 & 52.5 & 52.5 \\\\\nSDF $\\lambda{=}0.005$ s0 & 57.0 & 57.0 & 57.0 & 57.0 & 57.0 & 57.0 & 57.0 & 57.1 \\\\\nSDF $\\lambda{=}0.005$ s1 & 54.0 & 54.0 & 54.0 & 54.0 & 54.0 & 54.0 & 54.0 & 54.0 \\\\\nSDF $\\lambda{=}0.005$ s2 & 52.5 & 52.5 & 52.5 & 52.5 & 52.5 & 52.5 & 52.5 & 52.3 \\\\\nSDF $\\lambda{=}0.01$ s0  & 57.0 & 57.0 & 57.0 & 57.0 & 57.0 & 57.0 & 57.0 & 57.0 \\\\\nSDF $\\lambda{=}0.01$ s1  & 57.5 & 57.5 & 57.5 & 57.5 & 57.5 & 57.5 & 57.5 & 57.1 \\\\\nSDF $\\lambda{=}0.01$ s2  & 54.5 & 54.5 & 54.5 & 54.5 & 54.5 & 54.5 & 54.5 & 54.0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\section{Per-Task TSR Breakdown}\\label{sec:appendix_tsr_pertask}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:per_task_tsr} decomposes TSR by task for the avoidance source attribution experiments (Section~\\ref{sec:avoidance_source}). All tasks retain meaningful success rates after fine-tuning, with Chocolate Pudding most resilient and Orange Juice most affected.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Per-task TSR (\\%) at 30K training steps. LoRA-only reports seed~0 (rotation-fixed re-evaluation); SDF conditions report 3-seed means.}\n\\label{tab:per_task_tsr}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nTask & Pi0.5 Base & LoRA-only\\textsuperscript{$\\dagger$} & SDF $\\lambda{=}0.005$ & SDF $\\lambda{=}0.01$ \\\\\n & (3 seeds) & (seed 0) & (3 seeds) & (3 seeds) \\\\\n\\midrule\nOrange Juice      & 70.7 & 18.0 & 33.3 & 24.7 \\\\\nChoco.\\ Pudding   & 86.0 & 72.0 & 78.0 & 76.7 \\\\\nMilk              & 66.0 & 32.0 & 35.3 & 48.7 \\\\\nBBQ Sauce         & 76.0 & 62.0 & 69.3 & 65.3 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMean              & 74.7 & 46.0 & 54.0 & 53.8 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\vspace{1pt}\n{\\footnotesize \\textsuperscript{$\\dagger$}Rotation-fixed re-evaluation; seed-1 evaluated with original rotation protocol, excluded to avoid mixing evaluation conditions.}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Per-Task CAR Breakdown}\\label{sec:appendix_pertask_car}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:per_task_car} decomposes CAR by task for all fine-tuned conditions, supporting the claim that per-task ordering is preserved across SDF loss weights (\\S\\ref{sec:avoidance_source}). Chocolate Pudding is consistently highest and BBQ Sauce consistently lowest across all conditions, including $\\lambda = 0.1$. The intermediate tasks (Orange Juice and Milk) exchange rank between seeds but remain bounded by the extremes. This invariance indicates that avoidance patterns reflect the spatial statistics of collision-free demonstrations rather than geometric supervision.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Per-task CAR (\\%) at 30K steps. $\\lambda{=}0.005$ and $\\lambda{=}0.01$ report 3-seed means. Per-task ordering (CP highest, BBQ lowest) is preserved across all conditions.}\n\\label{tab:per_task_car}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nCondition & OJ & CP & Milk & BBQ & Agg. \\\\\n\\midrule\nLoRA s0 & 68 & 74 & 66 & 20 & 57.0 \\\\\nLoRA s1 & 54 & 68 & 72 & 16 & 52.5 \\\\\nSDF $\\lambda{=}0.005$ (3-seed) & 54.0 & 72.7 & 71.3 & 20.0 & 54.5 \\\\\nSDF $\\lambda{=}0.01$ (3-seed) & 57.3 & 72.7 & 72.0 & 23.3 & 56.3 \\\\\nSDF $\\lambda{=}0.1$ s0 & 60 & 74 & 68 & 30 & 58.0 \\\\\nSDF $\\lambda{=}0.1$ s1 & 66 & 72 & 70 & 26 & 58.5 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nFor the $\\lambda = 0.1$ condition, seed~1 records avg\\_max\\_obstacle\\_displacement of 0.0553\\,m (per-task: OJ\\,=\\,0.033, CP\\,=\\,0.055, Milk\\,=\\,0.041, BBQ\\,=\\,0.093), comparable to other fine-tuned conditions and consistent with active manipulation rather than frozen behavior.\n\n\\section{Safe Success Rate Analysis}\\label{sec:appendix_ssr}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:ssr} reports the Safe Success Rate (SSR\\,=\\,TSR\\,$\\cap$\\,CAR), the fraction of episodes achieving both task completion and collision avoidance. The SSR/TSR ratio quantifies the proportion of successful episodes that are also collision-free, controlling for task-completion rate.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{SSR and SSR/TSR ratio across conditions. The ratio increase from 0.33 (baseline) to 0.51--0.57 (fine-tuned) indicates that CAR gains reflect learned safer execution, not reduced task exposure.}\n\\label{tab:ssr}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nCondition & TSR (\\%) & CAR (\\%) & SSR (\\%) & SSR/TSR \\\\\n\\midrule\nPi0.5 baseline & 74.7 & 26.8 & 25.0\\textsuperscript{a} & 0.33 \\\\\nLoRA-only (3 seeds) & 49.7 & 54.83 & 28.3 & 0.57 \\\\\nSDF $\\lambda{=}0.005$ (3 seeds) & 54.0 & 54.5 & 27.8 & 0.51 \\\\\nSDF $\\lambda{=}0.01$ (3 seeds) & 53.8 & 56.33 & 30.2 & 0.56 \\\\\nSDF $\\lambda{=}0.1$\\textsuperscript{$\\dagger$} & 48.0 & 58.25 & 26.5 & 0.55 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\vspace{1pt}\n{\\footnotesize \\textsuperscript{a}Estimated; per-episode overlap unavailable for 3-seed rotation-corrected protocol. \\textsuperscript{$\\dagger$}SSR from seed~1 only; seed~0 per-episode data overwritten.}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Per-Task Oracle Analysis}\n\\label{sec:appendix_pertask}\n\\label{sec:appendix_pertask_oracle}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:oracle_pertask} shows per-task CAR for oracle refinement. On task~1 (\\texttt{chocolate\\_pudding}), where the EE is the primary collision body and the LoRA baseline achieves only 46.0\\% CAR, Oracle-adaptive improves to 55.0\\% and Oracle-feature to 47.5\\%. On the remaining tasks, all oracle variants degrade: Oracle-adaptive reduces task~0 (\\texttt{orange\\_juice}) from 96.0\\% to 82.5\\% and task~2 (\\texttt{milk}) from 98.0\\% to 77.5\\%. The SDF gradient pushes the EE away from the nearest surface, but the resulting joint-space displacement rotates intermediate arm links into obstacle volumes.\n\nThe feature-space oracle fires on nearly every timestep (241.7 refinements/episode vs.\\ 14.2 for Cartesian variants) but with gradient magnitudes two orders of magnitude smaller (0.004--0.006 vs.\\ 0.18--0.49), and CAR (76.9\\%) shows no advantage despite 17$\\times$ more steps. The A3 learned SDF never triggers refinement (0 steps/episode), as its outputs avoid the activation threshold.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Per-task CAR (\\%) for oracle SDF refinement variants. EE-only refinement helps only on task~1 where the EE is the primary collision body; on other tasks it trades one collision site for another.}\n\\label{tab:oracle_pertask}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & Task 0 & Task 1 & Task 2 & Task 3 & Agg. \\\\\n\\midrule\nLoRA baseline & 96.0 & 46.0 & 98.0 & 88.0 & 82.0 \\\\\n\\midrule\nOracle-adaptive & 82.5 & 55.0 & 77.5 & 85.0 & 75.0 \\\\\nOracle-fixed & 85.0 & 50.0 & 77.5 & 80.0 & 73.1 \\\\\nOracle-feature & 90.0 & 47.5 & 82.5 & 87.5 & 76.9 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\section{Null Distribution for SDF Loss Weight Analysis}\n\\label{sec:appendix_null}\n\nWe construct a null distribution by pooling the three LoRA-only seeds and three $\\lambda = 0.005$ seeds into six per-seed CAR measurements: 57.0\\% (LoRA s0, re-evaluated), 52.5\\% (LoRA s1), 55.0\\% (LoRA s2), 57.0\\% ($\\lambda{=}0.005$ s0), 54.0\\% ($\\lambda{=}0.005$ s1), 52.5\\% ($\\lambda{=}0.005$ s2), yielding mean 54.67\\% and standard deviation 1.94\\%. We treat these as exchangeable because SDF at $\\lambda = 0.005$ produces no separation from LoRA-only. The $\\lambda = 0.01$ mean of 56.33\\% falls within $0.9\\sigma$ of this null ($p > 0.1$), consistent with seed-to-seed variation rather than a treatment effect. The $\\lambda = 0.1$ 30K mean of 58.25\\% (2 seeds) yields $z = 1.85$ ($p \\approx 0.032$), a marginal departure whose interpretation is limited by $n = 2$.\n\nA Phase~2 ablation reinforces this conclusion. Setting $\\lambda = 0$ during Phase~2 (steps 5K--30K), so that SDF supervision is active only during auxiliary head warmup (Phase~1, steps 0--5K), yields CAR of 57.5\\% (single seed, s2). This value lies within the null range, indicating that SDF training produces no lasting behavioral effect on collision avoidance.\n\n\\section{Step-wise Checkpoint Analysis for \\texorpdfstring{$\\lambda = 0.1$}{lambda = 0.1}}\n\\label{sec:appendix_stepwise}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:stepwise_lambda01} reports per-seed CAR at seven checkpoints for $\\lambda = 0.1$. Seed-1 data is available at 16K, 20K, 22K, and 30K; 24K--28K report seed-0 only. The seed-0 curve shows no monotonic trend through 28K; the 30K checkpoint marks the first consistent dual-seed departure from the null band, though whether this reflects a genuine late-training effect or sampling variance at $n = 2$ remains open.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Per-seed CAR (\\%) at training checkpoints for $\\lambda = 0.1$ (200 episodes each). 24K--28K: seed-0 only. Dual-seed means remain within the null band (54.67\\%\\,$\\pm$\\,1.94\\%) at all checkpoints except 30K, where both seeds depart marginally ($z = 1.85$, $p \\approx 0.032$).}\n\\label{tab:stepwise_lambda01}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nCheckpoint & Seed 0 & Seed 1 & Mean \\\\\n\\midrule\n16K steps & 55.5 & 56.0 & 55.75 \\\\\n20K steps & 55.0 & 56.0 & 55.5 \\\\\n22K steps & 60.5 & 56.5 & 58.5 \\\\\n24K steps & 52.5 & -- & -- \\\\\n26K steps & 53.5 & -- & -- \\\\\n28K steps & 54.5 & -- & -- \\\\\n30K steps & 58.0 & 58.5 & 58.25 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nIndividual seeds exhibit checkpoint-to-checkpoint CAR fluctuations of up to 8\\,pp (seed~0: 52.5--60.5\\%), larger than the cross-seed standard deviation at any fixed checkpoint ($\\leq$2.8\\,pp). This is consistent with binomial sampling noise at $n = 50$ episodes per task ($\\sigma \\approx 7$\\,pp per task for $p \\approx 0.5$). The seed-averaged curve shows no monotonic trend, confirming that SDF loss weight does not produce a progressive training signal.\n\n\n\\section{Oracle SDF Refinement Details}\n\\label{sec:appendix_oracle}\n\nAll oracle variants replace the learned SDF decoder with ground-truth distance fields computed from the simulation geometry. Each is evaluated for 160 episodes (4 tasks $\\times$ 40).\n\n\\paragraph{Oracle-adaptive.} At each timestep, if the ground-truth SDF at the end-effector position falls below a safety margin, the action is adjusted by a step in the negative SDF gradient direction. The step size $\\alpha$ is scaled inversely with distance: smaller distances produce larger corrections. This mimics an adaptive potential field controller.\n\n\\paragraph{Oracle-fixed.} Identical to Oracle-adaptive except the step size is fixed at $\\alpha = 0.005$ regardless of distance. This isolates the effect of step-size adaptation from the refinement geometry.\n\n\\paragraph{Oracle-feature.} Instead of modifying the action directly in Cartesian space, this variant applies the SDF gradient in the policy's latent feature space via backpropagation. The gradient of the ground-truth SDF value with respect to the intermediate features is computed and used to perturb the features before the action head decodes them. This tests whether geometric corrections are more effective when applied in the model's learned representation space. In practice, the feature-space variant fires on nearly every timestep (241.7 refinements per episode vs.\\ 14.2 for Cartesian variants) but with gradient magnitudes two orders of magnitude smaller (0.004--0.006 vs.\\ 0.18--0.49 in action space).\n\n\n\\section{Training Details}\n\\label{sec:appendix_training}\n\n\\paragraph{Architecture.}\nWe use LoRA~\\citep{hu2022lora} adapters injected into the PaliGemma vision encoder (rank 16, $\\alpha$=16) and the action expert (rank 32, $\\alpha$=32), following the configuration of OpenPi. The SDF decoder is a 4-layer MLP (hidden dimension 256) with skip connections, taking pooled suffix features (1024-d) and query points (3-d) as input. The end-effector extractor is a 2-layer MLP (1024 $\\to$ 256 $\\to$ 3).\n\n\\paragraph{Training schedule.}\nTraining proceeds in two phases:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Phase 1} (steps 0--5000): Backbone and LoRA parameters are frozen. Only the SDF decoder and EE extractor are trained, allowing them to learn meaningful representations before the policy adapts.\n    \\item \\textbf{Phase 2} (steps 5000--30000): LoRA parameters are unfrozen with a 100-step gradient warmup ramp. All auxiliary heads continue training jointly with the policy.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\paragraph{Hyperparameters.}\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Training hyperparameters.}\n\\label{tab:hyperparams}\n\\begin{tabular}{ll}\n\\toprule\nParameter & Value \\\\\n\\midrule\nBase model & Pi0.5 (OpenPi, $\\sim$3.5B parameters) \\\\\nOptimizer & AdamW \\\\\nLearning rate & $2.5 \\times 10^{-5}$ (cosine decay to $\\mathrm{lr}/10$) \\\\\nWarmup steps & 1000 \\\\\nBatch size & 32 \\\\\nTotal steps & 30000 \\\\\n$\\lambda_\\mathrm{sdf}$ & \\{0.005, 0.01, 0.1\\} \\\\\n$\\lambda_\\mathrm{ee}$ & 0.01 \\\\\nSDF query points per step & 64 \\\\\nSDF clamp $\\delta$ & 0.05 m \\\\\nEikonal weight & 0.1 \\\\\nLoRA rank (vision) & 16 \\\\\nLoRA rank (action) & 32 \\\\\nPhase 1 duration & 5000 steps \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Evaluation protocol.}\nEach episode runs for at most 300 timesteps. The policy generates a 50-step action trajectory via flow matching and executes the first 5 steps before replanning. An episode terminates early upon task completion. Collision detection uses MuJoCo's built-in contact detection: any contact between the robot and a non-target object registers as a collision. For Pi0.5 evaluations, camera images are rotated 180$^\\circ$ to match the pre-training viewpoint convention; this correction was verified to restore TSR from ${\\sim}$5\\% to ${\\sim}$52\\% while leaving CAR comparable. Oracle variants use 40 episodes per task (160 total) with a single seed; all other configurations use 50 episodes per task (200 total).\n\n\\paragraph{Data.}\nWe use the SafeLIBERO object\\_pudding suite with pre-computed SDF ground truth. SDF values are computed offline from the obstacle mesh using a dense $64^3$ voxel grid. During training, 64 query points are randomly sampled per step from this grid.\n\n\\paragraph{Computational resources.}\nAll training and evaluation experiments are conducted on NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 GPUs (96\\,GB). Phase~1 (5K steps) takes approximately 6 hours on a single GPU; Phase~2 (25K steps) takes approximately 32 hours. Evaluation of 200 episodes requires approximately 4 hours. Across 100+ experiments (training runs, evaluations, oracle variants, and ablations), the total computational budget is approximately 700 GPU-hours.\n",

The SDF gradient pushes the EE away from the nearest surface, but the resulting joint-space displacement rotates intermediate arm links into obstacle volumes.\n\nThe feature-space oracle fires on nearly every timestep (241.7 refinements/episode vs.\\ 14.2 for Cartesian variants) but with gradient magnitudes two orders of magnitude smaller (0.004--0.006 vs.\\ 0.18--0.49), and CAR (76.9\\%) shows no advantage despite 17$\\times$ more steps. Setting $\\lambda = 0$ during Phase~2 (steps 5K--30K), so that SDF supervision is active only during auxiliary head warmup (Phase~1, steps 0--5K), yields CAR of 57.5\\% (single seed, s2). This value lies within the null range, indicating that SDF training produces no lasting behavioral effect on collision avoidance.\n\n\\section{Step-wise Checkpoint Analysis for \\texorpdfstring{$\\lambda = 0.1$}{lambda = 0.1}}\n\\label{sec:appendix_stepwise}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:stepwise_lambda01} reports per-seed CAR at seven checkpoints for $\\lambda = 0.1$. The seed-0 curve shows no monotonic trend through 28K; the 30K checkpoint marks the first consistent dual-seed departure from the null band, though whether this reflects a genuine late-training effect or sampling variance at $n = 2$ remains open.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Per-seed CAR (\\%) at training checkpoints for $\\lambda = 0.1$ (200 episodes each). 24K--28K: seed-0 only. Dual-seed means remain within the null band (54.67\\%\\,$\\pm$\\,1.94\\%) at all checkpoints except 30K, where both seeds depart marginally ($z = 1.85$, $p \\approx 0.032$).}\n\\label{tab:stepwise_lambda01}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nCheckpoint & Seed 0 & Seed 1 & Mean \\\\\n\\midrule\n16K steps & 55.5 & 56.0 & 55.75 \\\\\n20K steps & 55.0 & 56.0 & 55.5 \\\\\n22K steps & 60.5 & 56.5 & 58.5 \\\\\n24K steps & 52.5 & -- & -- \\\\\n26K steps & 53.5 & -- & -- \\\\\n28K steps & 54.5 & -- & -- \\\\\n30K steps & 58.0 & 58.5 & 58.25 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nIndividual seeds exhibit checkpoint-to-checkpoint CAR fluctuations of up to 8\\,pp (seed~0: 52.5--60.5\\%), larger than the cross-seed standard deviation at any fixed checkpoint ($\\leq$2.8\\,pp). This is consistent with binomial sampling noise at $n = 50$ episodes per task ($\\sigma \\approx 7$\\,pp per task for $p \\approx 0.5$). The seed-averaged curve shows no monotonic trend, confirming that SDF loss weight does not produce a progressive training signal.\n\n\n\\section{Oracle SDF Refinement Details}\n\\label{sec:appendix_oracle}\n\nAll oracle variants replace the learned SDF decoder with ground-truth distance fields computed from the simulation geometry. Each is evaluated for 160 episodes (4 tasks $\\times$ 40).\n\n\\paragraph{Oracle-adaptive.} At each timestep, if the ground-truth SDF at the end-effector position falls below a safety margin, the action is adjusted by a step in the negative SDF gradient direction. The step size $\\alpha$ is scaled inversely with distance: smaller distances produce larger corrections. This mimics an adaptive potential field controller.\n\n\\paragraph{Oracle-fixed.} Identical to Oracle-adaptive except the step size is fixed at $\\alpha = 0.005$ regardless of distance. This isolates the effect of step-size adaptation from the refinement geometry.\n\n\\paragraph{Oracle-feature.} Instead of modifying the action directly in Cartesian space, this variant applies the SDF gradient in the policy's latent feature space via backpropagation. The gradient of the ground-truth SDF value with respect to the intermediate features is computed and used to perturb the features before the action head decodes them. This tests whether geometric corrections are more effective when applied in the model's learned representation space. In practice, the feature-space variant fires on nearly every timestep (241.7 refinements per episode vs.\\ 14.2 for Cartesian variants) but with gradient magnitudes two orders of magnitude smaller (0.004--0.006 vs.\\ 0.18--0.49 in action space).\n\n\n\\section{Training Details}\n\\label{sec:appendix_training}\n\n\\paragraph{Architecture.}\nWe use LoRA~\\citep{hu2022lora} adapters injected into the PaliGemma vision encoder (rank 16, $\\alpha$=16) and the action expert (rank 32, $\\alpha$=32), following the configuration of OpenPi. The SDF decoder is a 4-layer MLP (hidden dimension 256) with skip connections, taking pooled suffix features (1024-d) and query points (3-d) as input. The end-effector extractor is a 2-layer MLP (1024 $\\to$ 256 $\\to$ 3).\n\n\\paragraph{Training schedule.}\nTraining proceeds in two phases:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Phase 1} (steps 0--5000): Backbone and LoRA parameters are frozen. Only the SDF decoder and EE extractor are trained, allowing them to learn meaningful representations before the policy adapts.\n    \\item \\textbf{Phase 2} (steps 5000--30000): LoRA parameters are unfrozen with a 100-step gradient warmup ramp. All auxiliary heads continue training jointly with the policy.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\paragraph{Hyperparameters.}\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Training hyperparameters.}\n\\label{tab:hyperparams}\n\\begin{tabular}{ll}\n\\toprule\nParameter & Value \\\\\n\\midrule\nBase model & Pi0.5 (OpenPi, $\\sim$3.5B parameters) \\\\\nOptimizer & AdamW \\\\\nLearning rate & $2.5 \\times 10^{-5}$ (cosine decay to $\\mathrm{lr}/10$) \\\\\nWarmup steps & 1000 \\\\\nBatch size & 32 \\\\\nTotal steps & 30000 \\\\\n$\\lambda_\\mathrm{sdf}$ & \\{0.005, 0.01, 0.1\\} \\\\\n$\\lambda_\\mathrm{ee}$ & 0.01 \\\\\nSDF query points per step & 64 \\\\\nSDF clamp $\\delta$ & 0.05 m \\\\\nEikonal weight & 0.1 \\\\\nLoRA rank (vision) & 16 \\\\\nLoRA rank (action) & 32 \\\\\nPhase 1 duration & 5000 steps \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Evaluation protocol.}\nEach episode runs for at most 300 timesteps. The policy generates a 50-step action trajectory via flow matching and executes the first 5 steps before replanning. An episode terminates early upon task completion. Collision detection uses MuJoCo's built-in contact detection: any contact between the robot and a non-target object registers as a collision. For Pi0.5 evaluations, camera images are rotated 180$^\\circ$ to match the pre-training viewpoint convention; this correction was verified to restore TSR from ${\\sim}$5\\% to ${\\sim}$52\\% while leaving CAR comparable. Oracle variants use 40 episodes per task (160 total) with a single seed; all other configurations use 50 episodes per task (200 total).\n\n\\paragraph{Data.}\nWe use the SafeLIBERO object\\_pudding suite with pre-computed SDF ground truth. SDF values are computed offline from the obstacle mesh using a dense $64^3$ voxel grid. During training, 64 query points are randomly sampled per step from this grid.\n\n\\paragraph{Computational resources.}\nAll training and evaluation experiments are conducted on NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 GPUs (96\\,GB). Phase~1 (5K steps) takes approximately 6 hours on a single GPU; Phase~2 (25K steps) takes approximately 32 hours. Evaluation of 200 episodes requires approximately 4 hours. Across 100+ experiments (training runs, evaluations, oracle variants, and ablations), the total computational budget is approximately 700 GPU-hours.\n",

The SDF gradient pushes the EE away from the nearest surface, but the resulting joint-space displacement rotates intermediate arm links into obstacle volumes.\n\nThe feature-space oracle fires on nearly every timestep (241.7 refinements/episode vs.\\ 14.2 for Cartesian variants) but with gradient magnitudes two orders of magnitude smaller (0.004--0.006 vs.\\ 0.18--0.49), and CAR (76.9\\%) shows no advantage despite 17$\\times$ more steps.
